I guess… it’s probably work that finally made me come in. I’m a physical scientist, and I’m usually pretty good at keeping track of experiments and meetings, but lately I just can’t keep my mind on the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing.

And then the thing everyone actually needed from me just… kind of falls apart.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since taking over that project, have you been feeling stressed by it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to treat it as normal project stress, because it’s a big responsibility and I don’t want to sound like I can’t handle it. I’d like to say yes, but… not completely. It’s most obvious with the spectroscopy project because that’s where the stakes are and everyone notices when I miss something.

I don’t mean to be careless, but I can lose hours, and then the experiment or the thing people actually needed from me is… not where it should be.\n\nSo, yes, it’s hard to resist, but it doesn’t feel like an intrusive thought exactly. Even in something like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours. I wish it did. I mean, it’s probably a little less obvious when I’m not at work because there isn’t an experiment sitting there waiting on me, but… no, I don’t think it just goes back to normal.

At work it shows up in this embarrassing way—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd old spectrum peak, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. I mean, I’ve been… unsettled, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s much of the time or just when I realize I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t really been tracking it as feeling on edge, if that makes sense. And then the thing everyone actually needed from me just… kind of falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dramatic. I know everyone gets distracted, but this feels like more than that, and I’m worried I’m not handling it well.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to childhood, were you already someone who had trouble staying focused or keeping track of tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I did fine in school overall, so I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t remember it being, like, a big obvious problem when I was a kid.\n\nI was probably a little scattered sometimes, but I think most kids are? I’d have to think about it more. Not like yesterday-recent, but… it’s been about seven months, since I took over this multi-site spectroscopy project. It’s been going on for about seven months, basically since I took over that multi-site spectroscopy project. I wouldn’t say I’m depressed, exactly… maybe a little worn down or discouraged sometimes. But I don’t want to overstate it. I can still do what I need to do, mostly. I mean, it shows up there the most because that project has so many moving pieces. I wouldn’t say I’m depressed, exactly… maybe a little worn down or discouraged sometimes. But I don’t want to overstate it. I can still do what I need to do, mostly. I mean, it shows up there the most because that project has so many moving pieces. I don’t mean to be careless, but I can lose hours, and then the experiment or the thing people actually needed from me is… not where it should be.\n\nSo, yes, it’s hard to resist, but it doesn’t feel like an intrusive thought exactly. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s probably work that finally made me come in. I’m a physical scientist, and I’m usually pretty good at keeping track of experiments and meetings, but lately I just can’t keep my mind on the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. And then the thing everyone actually needed from me just… kind of falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dramatic. I know everyone gets distracted, but this feels like more than that, and I’m worried I’m not handling it well.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to childhood, were you already someone who had trouble staying focused or keeping track of tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I did fine in school overall, so I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t remember it being, like, a big obvious problem when I was a kid.\n\nI was probably a little scattered sometimes, but I think most kids are? I’d have to think about it more. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, sure—work, whether I’m letting people down, normal life things. But I’m not sure it’s constant or across everything.\n\nMaybe I’m not the best judge of it. What do you mean by “many different things”?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying so much that it’s hard to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s hard to stop, exactly. I mean… sometimes I can get stuck on something for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “worrying” in the way you mean.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I can usually still function, I think. Maybe it depends on the day? Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble staying focused been a recent change for you?\n\nPatient: I think it is more recent, yes. Not like yesterday-recent, but… it’s been about seven months, since I took over this multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t know if that counts as a change or just the workload being different, but that’s when I really started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after taking over the multi-site spectroscopy project?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s been going on for about seven months, basically since I took over that multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to blame it all on the project, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m depressed, exactly… maybe a little worn down or discouraged sometimes. But I don’t want to overstate it. I can still do what I need to do, mostly. Is that what you mean by down?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I… I don’t think I’d call it sad, exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged or flat sometimes, but sad feels like a stronger word. I’m still trying to keep things together, so… I’m not sure if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do these concentration problems mainly happen when you’re dealing with the spectroscopy project?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean, it shows up there the most because that project has so many moving pieces. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s one thing I’m supposed to stay focused on, but then I’ll notice something like a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum… and suddenly I’m chasing that instead.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but I can lose hours that way, and then the actual thing people needed from me is late or kind of falling apart. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since taking over that project, have you been feeling stressed by it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to treat it as normal project stress, because it’s a big responsibility and I don’t want to sound like I can’t handle it. But it has gotten more stressful than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage on my own. Not because I don’t care—just… I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt strained enough that people were worried about the project and lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it has been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled off task, is it because an unwanted thought keeps intruding and feels hard to resist?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like an unwanted thought, I don’t think. It’s more like… something catches my attention and suddenly it feels important, or interesting, and I follow it before I realize I’ve left the actual task.\n\nLike on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or someone makes a side comment, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off trying to make sense of that. I don’t mean to be careless, but I can lose hours, and then the experiment or the thing people actually needed from me is… not where it should be.\n\nSo, yes, it’s hard to resist, but it doesn’t feel like an intrusive thought exactly. Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Outside of the spectroscopy project, is your concentration mostly normal?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, but… not completely. It’s most obvious with the spectroscopy project because that’s where the stakes are and everyone notices when I miss something.\n\nBut the real reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me is falling apart.\n\nSo it’s not only the project, exactly… it’s just where it causes the most damage. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work, does your concentration go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I wish it did. I mean, it’s probably a little less obvious when I’m not at work because there isn’t an experiment sitting there waiting on me, but… no, I don’t think it just goes back to normal.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that I can’t keep my mind on the actual thing in front of me anymore. At work it shows up in this embarrassing way—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd old spectrum peak, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. And then the thing everyone needed from me has kind of fallen apart.\n\nSo away from work it may look less serious, but I don’t feel like my concentration is normal. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been feeling tense or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “tense” exactly. I mean, I’ve been… unsettled, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s much of the time or just when I realize I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t really been tracking it as feeling on edge, if that makes sense. Should I be thinking more like physically tense, or worried in my head?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve really noticed it in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I still can enjoy things sometimes, I think… but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less than usual. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer just because I’m frustrated lately.\n\nDo you mean like hobbies and social things, or just in general?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, if that makes sense. I still do some of the things I normally like, but I haven’t been stopping to ask myself whether I’m enjoying them the same way.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Maybe I’m a little less pulled toward things lately, but I’m not sure if that’s “loss of interest” or just being distracted and tired of trying to keep up. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months, have you been worrying about many different parts of your life more days than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “many different parts” exactly. I don’t want to exaggerate it and make it sound like I’m constantly worrying about everything. How broad do you mean by “different parts”?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the day nearly every day. I’ve had some low moments, but I haven’t really been tracking whether it’s that constant.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like… some days I feel off or kind of weighed down, but I’m not sure it’s been all day, every day. Is that the level you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m still trying to keep things together, so… I’m not sure if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do these concentration problems mainly happen when you’re dealing with the spectroscopy project?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean, it shows up there the most because that project has so many moving pieces. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s one thing I’m supposed to stay focused on, but then I’ll notice something like a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum… and suddenly I’m chasing that instead.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but I can lose hours that way, and then the actual thing people needed from me is late or kind of falling apart. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since taking over that project, have you been feeling stressed by it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to treat it as normal project stress, because it’s a big responsibility and I don’t want to sound like I can’t handle it. But it has gotten more stressful than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage on my own. Not because I don’t care—just… I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt strained enough that people were worried about the project and lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it has been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled off task, is it because an unwanted thought keeps intruding and feels hard to resist?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like an unwanted thought, I don’t think. It’s more like… something catches my attention and suddenly it feels important, or interesting, and I follow it before I realize I’ve left the actual task.\n\nLike on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or someone makes a side comment, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off trying to make sense of that. I don’t mean to be careless, but I can lose hours, and then the experiment or the thing people actually needed from me is… not where it should be.\n\nSo, yes, it’s hard to resist, but it doesn’t feel like an intrusive thought exactly. Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Outside of the spectroscopy project, is your concentration mostly normal?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, but… not completely. It’s most obvious with the spectroscopy project because that’s where the stakes are and everyone notices when I miss something.\n\nBut the real reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me is falling apart.\n\nSo it’s not only the project, exactly… it’s just where it causes the most damage. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work, does your concentration go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I wish it did. I mean, it’s probably a little less obvious when I’m not at work because there isn’t an experiment sitting there waiting on me, but… no, I don’t think it just goes back to normal.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that I can’t keep my mind on the actual thing in front of me anymore. At work it shows up in this embarrassing way—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd old spectrum peak, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. And then the thing everyone needed from me has kind of fallen apart.\n\nSo away from work it may look less serious, but I don’t feel like my concentration is normal. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been feeling tense or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “tense” exactly. I mean, I’ve been… unsettled, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s much of the time or just when I realize I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t really been tracking it as feeling on edge, if that makes sense. Should I be thinking more like physically tense, or worried in my head?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve really noticed it in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I still can enjoy things sometimes, I think… but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less than usual. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer just because I’m frustrated lately.\n\nDo you mean like hobbies and social things, or just in general?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, if that makes sense. I still do some of the things I normally like, but I haven’t been stopping to ask myself whether I’m enjoying them the same way.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Maybe I’m a little less pulled toward things lately, but I’m not sure if that’s “loss of interest” or just being distracted and tired of trying to keep up. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months, have you been worrying about many different parts of your life more days than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “many different parts” exactly. I do worry, but I’m not sure if it’s been more days than not over the whole six months.\n\nIt’s more like certain things get in my head when they’re right in front of me, and then I move on or get busy. I don’t want to exaggerate it and make it sound like I’m constantly worrying about everything. How broad do you mean by “different parts”?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the day nearly every day. I’ve had some low moments, but I haven’t really been tracking whether it’s that constant.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like… some days I feel off or kind of weighed down, but I’m not sure it’s been all day, every day. Is that the level you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing an everyday task at home, do you often get sidetracked and leave it unfinished?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not just at home, actually. I try not to be dramatic about it, but that’s kind of why I came in.\n\nI’ll start something I’m supposed to be doing, and then some small thing pulls me away. At work it’s worse — like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or someone makes a side comment, or I see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me is just… not done.\n\nSo, yes, I guess I do get sidetracked and leave things unfinished. More than I’d like to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
